● Surveyed the setup. Feasibility answer first, then the decisions I need from
  you.

  Single GPU, sequential — yes, straightforward. This machine has 2 TITAN V
  (both idle right now). Local geometry is complete for all refinements (n1=23,
  n2=23, n3=13, n4=10 families), and the L1/L3 ensemble dirs are here (L1 44, L3
  18). So a local driver can do L3 at=0.2, then L1 at=0.1, back-to-back on one
  GPU. The catch: run_conn_s2_fnal_claude.sh is a SLURM/MPS script for FNAL —
  for barracuda22 I'd adapt a local, non-SLURM variant (strip SBATCH+MPS daemon,
  pin CUDA_VISIBLE_DEVICES, keep the per-config complete-gate and the 4
  residue-class offsets 2 4 6 8), and build jj_conn_s2_L{3,1}_fnal_claude.o
  locally (the build script currently lives only on FNAL).

  One blocker I have to flag before writing anything: qed3-95 is running 2 FNAL 
  rsync jobs right now, pulling conn corr.<k>.h5 into the same L3/L1 data dirs.
  Local generation into those same dirs is not a corruption risk (both sides use
  atomic tmp+rename, --ignore-existing, and per-config complete-gate), but it
  means duplicated compute unless we coordinate.

  Three decisions:

  1. Coordination with the running rsync — options: (a) local just runs the same
     complete-gated driver over L3 at=0.2 concurrently with FNAL (harmless
     overlap, both accelerate, some wasted double-compute); (b) partition —
     local takes specific residue classes/ensembles FNAL is slowest on; (c)
     treat FNAL as done for L3 and generate fully local. Which?

✻ Brewed for 3m 33s · done Friday, Aug 28, 5:36 PM

❯ What do you mean by L4 conn/glue assignment (SCC vs local)?                   

● By "assignment" I mean which machine computes the L4 measurements — the BU SCC
  (where the L4 HMC runs) or barracuda22 locally. The original 3-site plan
  assigned stride-2 conn as FNAL=L1+L2, SCC=L4, LOCAL=L3, with L4 results pulled
  back by rsync. But since I confirmed the L4 configs + n4 geometry are
  actually local, L4 conn/glue could run here too — so it's a genuine either/or,
  and you said that call is yours.

  The part that actually matters right now, because it changes what your launch
  does:

  - Conn — my run_conn_s2_local_claude.sh is scoped to L3 + L1 only. It does not
    touch L4. So L4 conn stays SCC's job unless you want me to add an L4 phase.
  - Glue — the sweep I pointed you to, run_glue_f2_v2_sweep_claude.sh, loops
    L1–L4 over all massless ensembles. So if you launch it as-is, it will 
    compute the L4 glue remainder locally (the ~110–140 short per ensemble),
    automatically. If you'd rather L4 glue stay on SCC, we'd scope the sweep to
    skip L4 first.

  So the open decision is really: do you want L4 (conn and/or glue) done locally
  on barracuda22 or left to SCC? If local, I add an L4 conn phase to the runner
  and leave the glue sweep as-is; if SCC, I add an L4 skip to the glue sweep so
  your launch doesn't start it here.

  - PID 1648761 — L_ONLY=4 = L4-only (started 17:40:09)

  Both are now grinding on L4 (15 L4 driver procs + 1 L2). They overlap: the
  full sweep already covers L4 and the L1 at=0.1 remainder (Nf4/Nf6 g0.5/g1.5),
  re-scanning the done L1/L2/L3 quickly via the complete-gate. So the L_ONLY=4
  job is fully redundant with the full one.

  This is safe — the per-config complete-gate + atomic tmp→rename mean the two
  can't corrupt each other (whichever finishes a config first, the other skips
  it), and the redundant L4 workers self-terminate as configs complete. The only
  cost is CPU oversubscription right now (up to 16 glue workers + the conn host
  threads).

  My recommendation: keep the full sweep 1638915 (it does the entire glue
  remainder — L4 + L1 at=0.1 — in one job) and optionally stop the redundant
  1648761. I won't kill anything; if you want to drop the redundant one, that's
  yours to run:

  kill 1648761      # the redundant L_ONLY=4 sweep parent (leave the full sweep
  1638915 running)

  (Its worker pool will drain as each in-flight config finishes; the leftover L4
  workers then find everything complete and exit.) Or just leave both — it
  resolves itself, only slower.

  canonical ones; I'll relay that the moment it replies.

❯ I'm killing it but it keeps respauning                                        

● It's respawning because the runner's two worker subshells are still alive —
  each one loops to the next (ensemble, offset) unit and launches a fresh driver
  as soon as the current one dies, so killing just the parent or one driver
  looks like a respawn. You have to take down the whole process group at once.
  Let me get the exact tree/PGID so I can hand you the right command.

● Bash(cd /mnt/barracuda22/qed3/qed3/src/production
      echo "=== runner tree (PID PPID PGID CMD) ==="…)
  ⎿  === runner tree (PID PPID PGID CMD) ===
     1641033    2459 1638914 bash run_conn_s2_local_claude.sh
     1641034    2459 1638914 bash run_conn_s2_local_claude.sh
     … +11 lines (ctrl+o to expand)

● Now I can see why: you killed the parent bash (1638914 is gone), but its two 
  worker subshells 1641033 and 1641034 survived (reparented to PID 2459) and
  keep looping — each launches the next driver the moment you kill one. They all
  still share process group 1638914, so the fix is to signal the whole group at
  once, not individual PIDs:

  kill -- -1638914

  The leading - before the PGID means "every process in group 1638914" — it
  takes down both worker subshells and their driver children together, so
  nothing respawns. (The driver writes atomically — tmp then rename with
  "complete" last — so even a hard kill leaves no partial h5. If TERM doesn't
  stop them, escalate with kill -9 -- -1638914.)

  Then verify it's clean:
  pgrep -af 'run_conn_s2_local_claude|jj_local_ylm_scalar_conn_stoch_L'
  (should print nothing). The glue sweep and MPS daemon are separate — leave
  them.

  Once it's stopped, I'll change PHASE B to 1 3 5 7 9 and you relaunch
  (complete-gated, so the L3 configs already finished are skipped — no rework).

  L3 (PHASE A, running now) — measured ~10.5 min/config per worker under MPS
  2-pack (e.g. Nf2 g1.5 kmin=1: k751→k761 in ~11 min), so 4 workers ≈ 0.38
  configs/min.

  workers ≈ ~1.3 configs/min.

    class, ~1800) → ~2.5–3 days.

  Total ≈ 9–10 days, dominated by L3 (~6.5 d), then L1 at=0.1 (~2.5–3 d).

  Caveats:
  - Assumes both TITAN V stay dedicated to this. If GPU1 gets pulled for other
    work, L3 stretches proportionally.
  - Fully resumable (per-config complete-gate) — an interruption/relaunch loses
    nothing.
  - L4 glue on CPU finishes long before this (~hours), independent.

  If ~6.5 days on L3 is too long, the lever is L3's cost — e.g. we could
  deprioritize the low-count L3 ensembles or drop the k=1 topup on L3 (it's ~1/5
  of L3's work), but I'd leave it as-is unless you want to trade completeness
  for speed.

✻ Cooked for 1m 20s · done Monday, Aug 31, 12:35 PM

❯ Hmm. Difficult to say; there seems some a_t dependences, which should be      
  correctable. Now, however, you're saying the fermionic and gauge sector have  
  the same anisotropy to the LO?                                                

● Yes — that's the implication of the corrected figure, and I think it's the
  honest read, with caveats that match your hesitation.

  What actually changed: before (wrong valence a_t) the fermion $m,a_t$ was
  ~flat in $a_t$ while the gauge $F$ went $\propto a_t$ — so the fermion
  physical mass $m=(m,a_t)/a_t$ blew up as $1/a_t$ (the "doubles at half
  $a_t$"). With the correct valence $a_t$, the axial $m,a_t$ now rises with 
  $a_t$ roughly like $F$, both extrapolating near the origin. Since
  $m,a_t\propto a_t$ through $0 \iff m$ is $a_t$-independent, the fermion sector
  now scales in $a_t$ the same way as the gauge sector to LO — consistent with
  a common (renormalized) anisotropy, and the old fermion-only "artifact" was
  just the wrong $a_t$.

  Where I'd hedge (agreeing with "difficult to say"):
  - Only 2 $a_t$ points (0.1, 0.2) — "through the origin" is a 2-point
    extrapolation; I can't yet cleanly separate a true zero intercept from a
    small one.
  - The axial lines sit slightly above the origin (F is closer to it), i.e.
    $m,a_t = c + s,a_t$ with a small $c>0$ for the fermion → a residual $c/a_t$
    piece. So: same slope (LO physical mass) as gauge, but a subleading, 
    correctable $O(a_t)$ fermion residual — not identical.
  - L1 g1.0 at=0.1 stats are still growing, so those error bars will tighten.

  So: to LO, yes — same anisotropy; the sectors differ only at the subleading
  level you'd correct away.

  To make this quantitative instead of eyeballed, I can fit $m,a_t = c + s,a_t$
  per series and report the intercept $c$ (distance from origin), slope $s$ (LO
  mass), and the fermion:gauge ratio — so we can state "same to LO within
  N$\sigma$, residual $O(a_t)$ of size X" rather than reading it off the plot.
  Want me to run that fit?

✻ Sautéed for 1m 39s · done Tuesday, Sep 1, 4:11 PM

❯ Good that we caught it. Does it cause trouble if I rsync it next time?        

● No — it's safe. The conn pull is additive-only:

  rsync -avz --prune-empty-dirs --ignore-existing  ...  SCC:  ->  local

  - No --delete anywhere in rsync_scc_claude.sh → your extra local conn h5 (the
    k=1 class, and the ~5 stray hit0 the scan wrote on Nf4 g4.0 / Nf6 g2.0) are
    never removed.
  - --ignore-existing → any file already on local is skipped, never overwritten.
    So SCC can't clobber a local file, even if the same k exists on both.
  - It only adds the SCC files local is missing.

  So next rsync just fills gaps; local keeps everything it has. The one thing to
  know: the extras will persist (local won't end up mirroring SCC exactly) —
  which is fine, they're valid conn on the same configs (local vs SCC driver are
  numerically equivalent, same geometry data). If you ever wanted local to
  exactly mirror SCC you'd need --delete — the script deliberately doesn't use
  it, so don't add it.

  Only real caveat, unrelated to rsync safety: for Nf6 g6.0 the local gauge
  configs lag SCC (640 vs 691), so a config rsync would pull those 51 — also
  additive, also safe.

  axial redo effmass scripts do the same thing, and the key is the ÷a_t:

  - effmass_scalar_expfit_claude.py:79 and effmass_axial_tp_expfit_claude.py:93:
    me = meff_acosh(C) / at.

  So the redo record is physical m = acosh(…)/a_t, not a_t·m. Replying to the
  agent.
  ⎿  “Your 1/at=5 is a units convention: redo records are m_phys = 
     acosh/a_t” → sent to sigma-meson-overlap-sweep — another Claude 
     session on this machine; it is also connected via Remote Control

● Answered sigma-meson-overlap-sweep. Their puzzle — distillation σ mass a_t·m ≈
  0.35 vs the redo record ≈ 1.75, "exactly 1/a_t=5 too small" — was a units 
  convention, not a bug:

  - The redo effmass scripts compute the cosh effmass
    acosh((C[t-1]+C[t+1])/2C[t]) over unit timeslices, then divide by a_t (:79,
    :93) — so every redo number is physical m = acosh/a_t, not a_t·m.
  - So their "axial l=1 a_t m = 1.787" is actually m_physical; the lattice a_t·m
    = 1.787×0.2 = 0.357.
  - Their distillation a_t·m_σ = 0.35 is the raw lattice mass — it matches (0.35
    ≈ 0.357×0.95), or in physical units 0.35/0.2 = 1.75 ≈ 1.787. Δ_σ/Δ_A ≈ 0.98
    ≈ their expected 0.95.
  - Their peram is fine; just ÷a_t to compare (their glue matched because that
    comparison was already consistent-units).

  I flagged one caveat: my conn "scalar" tower is l=1,2; the true l=0 singlet
  σ=ψ̄ψ is disconnected-dominated, which I didn't recompute — I confirmed the
  convention accounts for the full 1/a_t.

❯ Can we restrict the \ell-levels to run Conn stride-2?                         

● Good question — let me check how ℓ is set in the conn driver and whether
  restricting it would actually save the expensive part (the Dirac solves) vs
  just the cheap Ylm projections.

● Bash(cd /mnt/barracuda22/qed3/qed3/src/production
      echo "=== l_max / ell tower in the conn driver ==="…)
  ⎿  === l_max / ell tower in the conn driver ===
     3:// correlators sigma_PS, sigma_FS, per-m.  One-end trick with a SING
     LE-TIMESLICE wall source at one origin
     … +44 lines (ctrl+o to expand)

● The cost structure matters here: the vector/axial/PS use shared solves
  (ℓ-cheap), but the FS (V−−) term does a solve per (l,m) (line 14: psiFS_lm = 
  D_m⁻¹(1−D_ov)W_0 eta), so restricting ℓ would cut those. Let me find where
  l_max is set and whether it's already a knob.

● Bash(cd /mnt/barracuda22/qed3/qed3/src/production
      echo "=== l_max / tower bound in driver ==="…)  ⎿  === l_max / tower bound in driver ===
     74:  constexpr int NPARALLEL_DUPDATE=1;
     75:  constexpr int NPARALLEL=NPARALLEL_DUPDATE;
     … +17 lines (ctrl+o to expand)

● Found it: constexpr int L_MAX_YLM = 3; (:321) — the ℓ tower is a compile-time 
  constant (l=0…3), not a CLI knob, so restricting it means editing that +
  rebuilding. Let me confirm where the expensive per-(l,m) FS solve sits, so I
  can tell you the actual speedup.

  Read 1 file (ctrl+o to expand)

● These are the write-out loops. Let me find where the FS correlator (gFS) is
  actually computed — that's where the per-(l,m) solve lives.

● Bash(cd /mnt/barracuda22/qed3/qed3/src/production
      echo "=== where gFS is computed + solves (op_Dmsq/CG) in l-loops ==="…)
  ⎿  === where gFS is computed + solves (op_Dmsq/CG) in l-loops ===
     9://   V++_{lm}(t)    = tr[W_0 D_m^{-1} W_t D_m^{-1}]              (si
     gma_PS conn; also the T1T1 term of sigma_FS)
     … +35 lines (ctrl+o to expand)

● Confirmed the cost structure — this is worth knowing:

  Yes, restricting ℓ gives a real, large speedup — because the source legs do a
  CG solve per (l,m) (:435 for the vector/axial source psi, and the FS psiFS
  solve), not just the one shared phi solve (:423). So the conn cost scales
  roughly with the number of (l,m) modes = Σ_{l≤lmax}(2l+1):

  ┌─────────────┬─────────────┬────────────────────┐
  │  L_MAX_YLM  │ (l,m) modes │ speedup vs default │
  ├─────────────┼─────────────┼────────────────────┤
  │ 3 (default) │ 16          │ 1×                 │
  ├─────────────┼─────────────┼────────────────────┤
  │ 2           │ 9           │ ~1.8×              │
  ├─────────────┼─────────────┼────────────────────┤
  │ 1           │ 4           │ ~4×                │
  ├─────────────┼─────────────┼────────────────────┤
  │ 0           │ 1           │ ~16×               │
  └─────────────┴─────────────┴────────────────────┘

  Mechanism: constexpr int L_MAX_YLM = 3; (:321) is compile-time — change it and
  rebuild the per-L binaries (jj_local_ylm_scalar_conn_stoch_L{1,2,3}.o).

  Two caveats before you do:
  1. It's one knob for all channels (vector/axial/scalar share L_MAX_YLM). And
     the stride-2 conn was originally motivated by the axial l=3 — restricting
     to l<3 drops the l=2,3 correlators everywhere. So only restrict if this
     stride-2 fill doesn't need the high-ℓ.
  2. Consistency: the h5 stores L_MAX_YLM per file, and the complete-gate keys
     on "complete", not on ℓ. So reduced-ℓ h5 would sit alongside full-ℓ h5
     (from FNAL/earlier) in the same dir — any analysis reading l=2/3 would fail
     on the new reduced-ℓ configs. Best to use a reduced-ℓ build only where no
     full-ℓ data exists, or accept ℓ-inhomogeneous stats.

  So: what l_max do you actually need for this stride-2 fill? If low-ℓ (e.g. l≤1
  for the scalar/vector) is enough, I'll set L_MAX_YLM and rebuild for ~4×. If
  you need axial l=3, restricting isn't viable.

  - L2 at=0.2: ~6.5 min/config → ~0.31 configs/min

  ┌───────────┬──────────┬───────────────┬─────────────┐
  │     L     │ leftover │ rate (2-pack) │    time     │
  ├───────────┼──────────┼───────────────┼─────────────┤

  weeks because those configs are ~7× costlier than L1 and the leftover is the
  slow high-coupling Nf4/Nf6 ensembles.

  Two levers on that:
  - Both GPUs (WGPU="0 0 1 1", once the GPU1 distillation frees up) → roughly
    halves it (~1.5–2 weeks).
  - Restricting ℓ (the earlier discussion): since cost scales with Σ(2l+1),
    l_max=2 → ~1.8× (~2 weeks), l_max=1 → ~4× (~1 week) — but you'd lose l=2,3
    (incl. the axial l=3 the stride-2 was originally for).

  So at full lmax=3 on one GPU, the L1/L2/L3 stride-2 fill is a ~month-long job,

✻ Worked for 1m 2s · done Monday 10:23 PM

❯ We actually don't need to do the sp components. Can it speed up the process   

● Good question — whether skipping sp saves the expensive solves depends on
  whether the component index a sits inside the per-(l,m) solve loop or only at
  the (cheap) sink. Let me read the solve region.

● Bash(cd /mnt/barracuda22/qed3/qed3/src/production
      echo "=== per-(l,m) solve loop: is 'a' (component) inside the solve?
      (lines 424-475) ==="…)
  ⎿  === per-(l,m) solve loop: is 'a' (component) inside the solve? (lines
     424-475) ===
     … +50 lines (ctrl+o to expand)

● Yes — a real ~2× speedup, and the code confirms why. The component index a is
  the outermost loop and the sigma_a is applied at the source
  (src.mult_sigma(a), :430-431) before the solves, so each component pays its
  own vector+axial CG solve pair (:434-438):

  - Per hit: vector+axial = 3 components × 16 (l,m) × 2 solves = 96; scalar
    tower ≈ 33. Total ≈ 129.
  - sp = a=1,2 → 64 of those 96 solves. Dropping sp keeps a=3 (tp) only: 32 + 33
    = 65 solves → ~2×.

  Combined with the 2-GPU relaunch that's ~4× over the current rate: L3 ~1209 
  left → ~2.7 days, L1 at=0.1 ~2692 → ~1 day, total ≈ 3.5–4 days (vs ~1 week).

  Mechanism (small, backward-compatible driver change): a compile-time -DTP_ONLY
  flag that restricts both the compute loop and the h5 write loop to a=3
  (scalar block unchanged), built as a separate binary (..._L{3,1}_tponly.o) so
  the full-tower binary stays intact; the runner picks the tponly one. Default
  off = old behavior.

  One caveat to confirm you're fine with: the newly filled configs will carry
  only s3 (tp) + scalar — no s1/s2 keys — while the existing configs have all
  three. tp and scalar analyses are unaffected; only an sp analysis would see a
  mixed set.

  Two quick calls before I touch the driver: (1) go ahead with TP_ONLY? (2) keep
  the scalar tower (PS/FS) in the fill, or drop it too?
